I'm just... dealing with the fact that I end up snapping when I'm in those situations. But no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding people or hiding out.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've always had a bit of a perfectionistic streak or high expectations for yourself and others?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I expect people to do their jobs, especially when safety is on the line, but I wouldn't say I'm a perfectionist. I've never been one to micromanage or get hung up on every little detail. I'm normally pretty reasonable to work for.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say anxious, no. I mean... I don't have all the exact technical details on why it blew yet, if that's what you're looking for. We're still sorting that out at the plant. But yeah... that's pretty much it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep since these episodes of snapping at people started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my sleep has been pretty bad. Most nights I just lie there, and it's like I can still hear the alarm going off in my head. When I do finally manage to doze off, I end up waking up sweating. I keep having these dreams about smoke and people yelling... so by the time morning rolls around, I feel like I haven't slept at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. It's... well, any time I hear a forklift beep, or a compressor kick on, or even just the smoke alarm chirping at the house, my whole body just tightens right up. I immediately start looking around for exits, trying to figure out what's gonna go wrong next. It's like I just can't turn that switch off, no matter where I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say it happens for no reason. Like I just mentioned, it's usually a specific sound or something happening around me that sets it off. If I'm just sitting at home, and it's completely quiet, and there's nothing going on... I don't really feel wound up. I mostly just feel exhausted. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm on edge when there's absolutely nothing going on. I mean, I feel bad right after I snap at my wife or the kids, obviously, because they don't deserve it. But... overall, like a deep sense of guilt about something else? No, I wouldn't say so. It's just the frustration in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been someone who tends to express your emotions openly, or is this something that's changed recently?\n\nPatient: No, this is completely new for me. I've always been pretty even-keeled. That's actually why I'm here. I keep blowing up at people over absolutely nothing. I'm snapping at my team on operations calls, and like I said, I'm doing it to my family, too. That is just not how I've ever handled things before. It's really out of character for me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these episodes of snapping at people?\n\nPatient: It's been about three weeks. Basically, it started right after the valve failure and the injury at the facility. Ever since then, I've just been... like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or interactions because of how you might react?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been doing that. I mean, I can't exactly avoid my team or my family. I still have a job to do and a house to live in, so I'm still showing up for all the normal stuff. I'm just... dealing with the fact that I end up snapping when I'm in those situations. But no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding people or hiding out.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've always had a bit of a perfectionistic streak or high expectations for yourself and others?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I expect people to do their jobs, especially when safety is on the line, but I wouldn't say I'm a perfectionist. I've never been one to micromanage or get hung up on every little detail. I usually just trust my guys to get the work done. So no, I wouldn't say I've ever had crazy high expectations or anything like that. I'm normally pretty reasonable to work for.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say anxious, no. I mean... I'm not sitting around worrying or feeling nervous, if that's what you mean. It's more just this short fuse I've got going on right now. But as far as just feeling anxious... no, I haven't really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the valve failure and the injury at the facility - can you tell me more about what happened during that incident?\n\nPatient: I mean... there's not really much more to it than what I said. The valve just gave out. It failed, and one of my guys took the brunt of it and got hurt. It all happened really fast. I don't have all the exact technical details on why it blew yet, if that's what you're looking for. We're still sorting that out at the plant. But yeah... that's pretty much it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep since these episodes of snapping at people started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my sleep has been pretty bad. Most nights I just lie there, and it's like I can still hear the alarm going off in my head. When I do finally manage to doze off, I end up waking up sweating. I keep having these dreams about smoke and people yelling... so by the time morning rolls around, I feel like I haven't slept at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. It's... well, any time I hear a forklift beep, or a compressor kick on, or even just the smoke alarm chirping at the house, my whole body just tightens right up. I immediately start looking around for exits, trying to figure out what's gonna go wrong next. It's like I just can't turn that switch off, no matter where I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say it happens for no reason. Like I just mentioned, it's usually a specific sound or something happening around me that sets it off. If I'm just sitting at home, and it's completely quiet, and there's nothing going on... I don't really feel wound up. I mostly just feel exhausted. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm on edge when there's absolutely nothing going on. It usually takes something to trigger it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're constantly scanning your surroundings for potential threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: I think I just answered that, actually. Like I was saying a minute ago, yeah, I do. If I hear a noise that sounds like the warehouse—a beep, a loud motor, something like that—I'm immediately looking around, checking for the exits, trying to spot the danger. It happens at the grocery store, at home... pretty much anywhere if something sets me off. So, yeah, I am scanning my surroundings a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any memories or scenes from the valve failure incident that feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely have. I could just be sitting at the kitchen table, or trying to answer some emails from home, and... suddenly I'm right back there. I see the flash from that valve blowing, and the smoke filling up the aisle. It's like it just shoves everything else right out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or situations that you're avoiding because they remind you of the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I haven't gone back to work yet, obviously, but other than that... I haven't been avoiding anywhere specific. I still go to the grocery store, I still run errands. Like I said, it's more about the sounds setting me off when I'm out, rather than the places themselves. I'm not avoiding going out or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were outside of your body watching yourself during one of these episodes of snapping at people?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I've never felt like I was outside my body or watching myself. When I snap at people, I'm definitely the one doing it. I'm just angry and on edge, but I'm fully in my own head the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were in a daze or disconnected from what was happening around you after the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really felt disconnected or... like I'm in a daze. I'm actually pretty aware of everything going on around me most of the time. Like I was saying before, I'm mostly just on edge, but I'm definitely present. So, no, I haven't noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the valve failure incident?\n\nPatient: I think we kind of just touched on this, but no. Other than not going back to work yet, I haven't been avoiding any specific activities. I'm still doing my normal routine... running errands, getting things done. Like I said, it's really just the sudden noises that mess with me, not the actual things I'm doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
